41: Programming framework (Phase C):

45: * in the ship item NBT at its top level (the assembler writes it from the controller block's stored program at

55: * the 15×15×15 base grid + derived stats + program), read with {@link #readBase}. The blueprint item itself
56: * stays in the gateway's blueprint slot (reusable — the ship carries a data copy).

62: * {@code partsList()} keys ({@code block.<NAME>} / {@code cover.<NAME>}). The CONSTRUCT handler credits the
63: * site part by part; whatever the ship carries beyond the site's remaining needs is discarded.

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\ship\VoidcraftRole.java ====
8: * Voidcraft roles, derived from which capability stats the ship has.
9: *
10: * <p>
11: * A ship whose stats contribute to exactly one role is "dedicated" (100% efficiency for that role). Every
12: * additional active role is a hybridization and applies the {@link VoidcraftConstants#HYBRID_ROLE_PENALTY}
13: * multiplier again (multiplicative). The role set and the penalty are the ship-level "role / hybrid penalty"
14: * output required by the implementation plan.
23: * Bitmask of all four roles.
43: * @param roleBitmask packed role mask (see {@link #getBit()})
44: * @return true if this role is active

54: * @param scanPower         total scan power
55: * @param constructionPower total construction power
56: * @param starlifterPower   total starlifter power
57: * @return packed role mask (0 = no role, i.e. a pure transport/hauler ship)
69: * @param roleBitmask packed role mask
70: * @return number of active roles (0-4)
81: * Hybrid penalty: efficiency multiplier for a ship with {@code activeRoleCount} roles.
82: *
83: * <p>
84: * 0 roles (pure hauler) and 1 role (dedicated) both get 1.0 — the penalty only kicks in from the second active
85: * role onward, matching "dedicated ships are most efficient, hybrids pay per extra role".
86: *
87: * @param activeRoleCount number of active roles
88: * @return efficiency multiplier in (0, 1]
98: * @param roleBitmask packed role mask
99: * @return the active roles in enum order (never null)
==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\ship\VoidcraftStats.java ====
4: * Immutable stat block of a digitized Voidcraft.
5: *
6: * <p>
7: * Produced by {@link VoidcraftBlueprint} from the component and cover grid.
8: *
9: * <p>
10: * <b>Thrust model (pass 18, pass 23 covers-only, pass 24 flip).</b> Thrust is a <em>single value</em>: the sum of
11: * the thrust of every {@code THRUSTER_NOZZLE} cover that faces the ship's BACK (exhaust out the rear, −Z = the
12: * assembler side; the nose is the far end, grid +Z). Nozzles aimed anywhere else contribute nothing; there is no
13: * net vector and no cancellation.
14: *
15: * <p>
16: * {@code speed} is derived, not additive: {@code max(0, thrust / mass)} — the thrust-to-mass ratio, unclamped.
17: * Travel time to a destination is an actual measure of DISTANCE divided by this ratio:
18: * {@code USSConstants.travelTicks(distance, speed)} = {@code distance · 20 / speed} (clamped to [20, 600] ticks),
19: * so a high thrust/mass ratio (or a closer destination) means a short leg.
24: * Single thrust value: sum of thrust of all back-facing thruster nozzles (pass 23: covers only).
26: * Derived: max(0, thrust / mass) — the thrust-to-mass ratio, unclamped.
35: * Energy generated per game tick (the solar panel covers; 0 for a plain ship).
57: * Speed derivation shared by all stat math: thrust divided by mass (the thrust-to-mass ratio), clamped at 0
58: * but NOT at 1 — a strong engine load legitimately out-masses the old scale, and
59: * {@link tectech.voidcraft.uss.USSConstants#travelTicks(double, double)} (the [20, 600] tick window) is what
60: * bounds the resulting travel time.
61: *
62: * @param thrust total thrust (sum of back-facing engines)
63: * @param mass   total mass
64: * @return speed = max(0, thrust / mass)
==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\CargoHold.java ====
13: * The internal cargo of a Voidcraft (the cargo-capacity pass): a bounded hold that the ship's mining, starlifting
14: * and construction fill, and that limits what the ship can carry.
15: *
16: * <p>
17: * <strong>Capacity model.</strong> The hold has a fixed capacity in <em>cargo units</em>, where
18: * {@code 1 unit = 1 item = 100 mB of fluid} (user spec). Items consume their count in units; fluids consume
19: * {@code mB / 100} units. Adding cargo is clamped by the remaining capacity — a full hold simply stops accepting
20: * more (the ship "cannot mine if it is full").
21: *
22: * <p>
23: * <strong>Generic framework.</strong> The hold is the single source of truth for a Voidcraft's cargo, with a small
24: * immutable API: {@link #addItems}/{@link #addFluids} (fill), {@link #removeItems}/{@link #removeFluids} (empty),
25: * and {@link #transferTo} (the generic ship-to-ship transfer primitive — the gameplay around it is not implemented
26: * yet, but this is the cargo-level operation it will use). All operations return a NEW instance (the hold is
27: * immutable), so the ship's current hold is never mutated in place.
28: *
29: * <p>
30: * Bare-JVM safe: only {@link Materials} data + plain long amounts (no Forge {@code Fluid}/{@code FluidStack}/
31: * {@code ItemStack} objects), so the capacity math is unit-testable without a live ore dictionary or fluid registry.
32: * The item/fluid resolution happens at the delivery boundary (the bay), like {@link USSShipCargo}.
36: * 1 cargo unit = 100 mB of fluid (user spec: "1 cargo unit = 1 item = 100L fluid").
39: NBT tags (voidcraft "vc_" naming convention).
46: * The hold's capacity in cargo units (1 unit = 1 item = 100 mB).
49: * Items on board: GT material → item count.
52: * Fluids on board: GT material → mB count.
62: * @param capacity the hold's capacity in cargo units (1 unit = 1 item = 100 mB; negative clamped to 0)
63: * @return a fresh, empty hold with the given capacity
70: * @return an empty hold with ZERO capacity (the defensive default — holds nothing)
76: region queries
79: * @return the hold's capacity in cargo units
86: * @return the used capacity in cargo units — the item count plus {@code mB / 100} for each fluid
100: * @return the remaining capacity in cargo units (never negative)
107: * @return true when the hold is at (or beyond) its capacity — the ship cannot take on more cargo
114: * @return true when the hold carries no items and no fluids
121: * @param material the ore material (null → 0)
122: * @return the item count on board for the material (0 when absent)
133: * @param material the fluid material (null → 0)
134: * @return the mB count on board for the material (0 when absent)
145: * @return an unmodifiable view of the item counts (material → count)
152: * @return an unmodifiable view of the fluid amounts (material → mB)
158: endregion
160: region modifications (immutable — each returns a new hold)
163: * Add items to the hold, clamped by the remaining capacity (1 item = 1 unit).
164: *
165: * @param material the item material (null / _NULL → this hold unchanged)
166: * @param count    the item count to add (≤ 0 → this hold unchanged)
167: * @return a new hold with the (clamped) items added
183: * Add fluids to the hold, clamped by the remaining capacity (100 mB = 1 unit).
184: *
185: * @param material the fluid material (null / _NULL → this hold unchanged)
186: * @param mB       the mB amount to add (≤ 0 → this hold unchanged)
187: * @return a new hold with the (clamped) fluids added
203: * Remove items from the hold (clamped by what is on board).
204: *
205: * @param material the item material (null / _NULL → this hold unchanged)
206: * @param count    the item count to remove (≤ 0 → this hold unchanged)
207: * @return a new hold with the (clamped) items removed
228: * Remove fluids from the hold (clamped by what is on board).
229: *
230: * @param material the fluid material (null / _NULL → this hold unchanged)
231: * @param mB       the mB amount to remove (≤ 0 → this hold unchanged)
232: * @return a new hold with the (clamped) fluids removed
252: endregion
254: region transfer (the generic ship-to-ship primitive)
257: * The result of a transfer: the (updated) source and target holds.
261: * The source hold after the transfer (cargo removed).
264: * The target hold after the transfer (cargo added, clamped by its capacity).
274: * Transfer as much cargo as possible from this hold to another hold — the generic Voidcraft-to-Voidcraft
275: * transfer primitive. Items transfer 1-for-1; fluids transfer in whole 100 mB units. The transfer is clamped by
276: * the TARGET's remaining capacity (the target's capacity is the binding constraint), so a full target simply
277: * stops accepting more (the source keeps the remainder).
278: *
279: * <p>
280: * The gameplay around this (which ship transfers to which, when, and the transfer's tick cost) is NOT
281: * implemented — this is only the cargo-level operation it will build on.
282: *
283: * @param target the hold to transfer into (null → a no-op result: this hold as source, no target)
284: * @return the (updated) source + target holds
292: Items: transfer each material's count, clamped by the target's remaining capacity (1 item = 1 unit).
305: Fluids: transfer in whole 100 mB units, clamped by the target's remaining capacity (100 mB = 1 unit).
321: endregion
323: region NBT
326: * Serialize the hold (capacity + items + fluids).
327: *
328: * @param nbt the compound to write into (null → no-op)
357: * Restore a hold from NBT.
358: *
359: * @param nbt the tag written by {@link #writeToNBT(NBTTagCompound)} (null → an empty zero-capacity hold)
360: * @return the restored hold (never null)
393: endregion
==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\MTEUnstableSolarSystem.java ====
92: * Unstable Solar System (EoH rework, Phase 2 vertical slice).
93: *
94: * <p>
95: * A new machine parallel to the legacy Eye of Harmony (the legacy code stays untouched, plan §1.1): the shell is
96: * the legacy sphere scaled 2× about its center (pass 12: 65×65×65 — same casings and field generators, same hatch
97: * rules, same per-type block counts, anchor at the scaled {@code ~} slot 32,32,0) — but instead of mining planets
98: * for star matter it hosts an <em>ignitable star</em>:
99: * <ul>
100: * <li>COLD until a {@link ItemUSSController} is inserted into the controller slot;</li>
101: * <li>IGNITED — the star burns: the render block is placed and the lifespan from {@link USSConstants} is counted
102: * down every machine tick. The spacetime compression field tier decides the star class and the lifespan;</li>
103: * <li>lifespan reaches zero — the star burns out: render block removed, the controller item is consumed (one
104: * controller = one star life), state returns to COLD.</li>
105: * </ul>
106: *
107: * <p>
108: * This slice deliberately has <b>no energy consumption</b> (the global energy model lands in Phase 6;
109: * {@link USSConstants#starDrawEUt(int)} holds the placeholder table) and no recipes
110: * ({@link #checkProcessing_EM()} returns NO_RECIPE — this is a state machine, not a recipe machine). The legacy EoH
111: * keeps working unchanged in the same world.
112: *
113: * @see VoidcraftUSS
114: * @see USSConstants
115: * @see ItemUSSController
116: * @see docs/Voidcraft_Implementation_Plan.md
122: * Structure piece name (same as the legacy EoH — the shell is identical).
125: Region variables (same field names as the legacy EoH — the structure callbacks below set them).
133: * Render-block animation on/off (screwdriver toggle, persisted in NBT).
136: * Star lifecycle model (persisted in NBT — the state survives chunk/world reloads).
140: * Voidbase construction sites (one per anchor — a Constructor's CONSTRUCT leg creates or fills them; a
141: * completed site spawns the base and is removed). Persists in NBT; discarded on burnout/teardown like the
142: * ships.
147: * The completed Voidbases (one per anchor; each ticks itself — integrity decay/repair, anchor hover, energy).
148: * Persisted in NBT; discarded on burnout/teardown like the ships.
153: * The base pilots — one per completed Voidbase (index-parallel to {@link #bases}): each base runs its
154: * station program (the digitized controller program) in BASE mode against this MTE (the same seam as the
155: * ships).
160: * The fleet render signature last pushed to the fleet anchor (Phase D): a hash of the ship count + every
161: * base integrity + every site progress. {@link #tickBases()} resyncs the anchor exactly when it changes
162: * (integrity decay/repair, site fill), so the client tint/fill follows the server without per-tick packets.
167: * The system's GATEWAYS, registered by the {@code MTEVoidcraftGateway} machines that locked onto this USS (each
168: * re-registers on its launch-target scan). Keyed by world-block coords, value the same coords. This is the
169: * server-side source of the gateway list the fleet anchor renders (a permanent part of the system view, even
170: * with an empty fleet). Pruned of destroyed gateways (see {@link #pruneGateways(int[])}). In-memory: a world
171: * reload simply re-runs the gateways' launch-target scans and re-registers.
175: Region mining mission (Phase 4 pass 5 — up to USSConstants.MAX_SHIPS_PER_USS ships in flight per USS; a large
176: fleet (dozens–hundreds) rendered by ONE fleet anchor block, not one block per ship).
179: * The ships in flight; list index = ship SLOT (launch order). Each ship carries its own cargo (built when its
180: * MINING leg completes) and its own return targets (captured at launch), so a mission from any gateway/bay pair
181: * is routed back to its own launchers.
186: * The pilots — one per in-flight ship (programming framework, Phase C), index-parallel to {@link #activeShips}.
187: * Each pilot runs its ship's program (the controller's instruction list) against this MTE (the
188: * {@link USSPilotWorld} game seam) and decides the ship's legs; the ships themselves are passive leg drivers.
193: * The state id last pushed to each slot's ship render TE (avoids per-tick description packets). Starts at -1
194: * ("nothing pushed yet"); on NBT load it is set to each restored ship's current state — the slot's render TE
195: * already holds it (it is a separate world block with its own NBT), so no re-push is needed after a reload.
206: * The leg id last pushed to each slot's ship render TE (programming framework, Phase C): the leg ID

2491: * UUID) + state + seed (the per-launch identity key, pass 5.1) + mission target (pass 7) + the
2492: * anchor-relative gateway (each mission routes back to its own launcher/bay, Phase 4 pass 4).

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSBasePilot.java ====
11: * A Voidbase PILOT (Voidbase construction framework): runs the station controller program (the one digitized
12: * into the blueprint) in BASE mode - the base does not fly. It mirrors the in-flight {@link USSShipPilot}: the
13: * same executor, the same command handlers, the same failure-SKIP contract (and the same invisible-while
14: * program wrap), but the legs are
15: * ZERO-LENGTH (a base sits at its anchor; a leg completes on the next tick):
16: * <ul>
17: * <li>MOVE resolves only to the base OWN anchor (an instant leg in place); any other target - and HOME / SHIP -
18: * is unresolvable (SKIP).</li>
19: * <li>MINE with mining power runs a REAL mining leg (the ship's duration table: {@code mineTicks(mining power)});
20: * MINE without mining power is an instant no-op leg (v1: a station produces no cargo). SCAN and SIPHON are
21: * instant no-op legs on a base (a station cannot scan or siphon - those yields belong to the ships).</li>
22: * <li>REPAIR runs at the station (energy from its own buffer).</li>
23: * <li>CONSTRUCT SKIPs (a base carries no parts - a build loadout belongs to the Constructor in flight).</li>
24: * <li>a non-empty program runs forever (the executor's invisible while) - it ends only on a STOP (or an empty
25: * program); at the end the base HOLDS its course (it stays; only integrity decay decommissions it).</li>
26: * </ul>
27: *
28: * <p>
29: * Bare-JVM except the world seam (like the ship pilot).
33: * The pilot NBT key nested inside the base NBT (same pattern as the ship pilot).
50: The leg (zero-length on a base - completes on the next tick; the latch is consumed exactly once).
53: * The leg in flight (or the last one)'s work kind (see {@link USSWorkKind}; TRAVEL = a travel leg).
57: The active mining leg (a WORK on a base with mining power): ticks left, the leg's total, and the leg id
58: (monotonic; the client animates the mining beam from it). 0 = no active mining leg.

90: * COMPLETED - the base holds, never a half-run).

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSCapabilities.java ====
4: * The COMMAND CAPABILITY SET of a ship (or base) — which program commands the underlying craft can actually run
5: * (the capability system: a program view only offers what the craft can do).
6: *
7: * <p>
8: * One bit per command family; the always-available commands (WRITE / READ / WAIT / STOP and the flow blocks)
9: * need no bit. The capability set is DERIVED from the craft's stats (the digitized item knows them exactly) or,
10: * for the in-world controller editor, from the covers mounted on the controller block itself (an empty
11: * controller says nothing about the hull, so it reports {@link #UNIVERSAL}).
12: *
13: * <p>
14: * The editor rejects inserts / preset applications outside the set; the RUNTIME enforces the truth on top (the
15: * pilots refuse a work leg the craft has no power for, and a REPAIR on a ship SKIPs) — the capability set gates
16: * the authoring surface, the pilots gate execution.
17: *
18: * <p>
19: * Bare JVM (plain int bits — the GUI builds it fresh on both sides from the same source data; no sync of its own).
23: * The craft has thrusters (speed &gt; 0) — the MOVE command.
25: * The craft has mining power — the MINE command.
27: * The craft has scan power — the SCAN command.
29: * The craft has siphon (starlifter) power — the SIPHON command.
31: * The craft has construction power — the CONSTRUCT command.
33: * The craft carries a repair bay — the REPAIR command (stations only; a ship's REPAIR always SKIPs).
36: * Every capability.
45: * @param bits arbitrary bit set (masked to {@link #ALL}).
50: * No command at all (only the always-available flow commands).
55: * Every command (an empty controller block — nothing is known about the hull).
93: * Whether a command may be INSERTED into a program under this capability set. The always-available commands
94: * (WRITE / READ / WAIT / STOP) are allowed unconditionally; an unknown id is allowed here (the executor SKIPs
95: * unregistered ids at run time).
116: * The capability bit a preset requires (0 = none — {@code clear} needs nothing).
136: * @return true when the preset may be applied under this capability set (a preset needing nothing is always
137: *         allowed).
==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSCommand.java ====
4: * Stable ids of the built-in Voidcraft commands (programming framework).
5: *
6: * <p>
7: * Programs reference commands by these ids (see {@link USSNode#command(int, net.minecraft.nbt.NBTTagCompound)});
8: * the command REGISTRY that maps ids to handlers is Phase B (the modularity seam — a new command is one new
9: * handler plus an id here; the program format never changes).
13: * Highest built-in command id (the GUI's stat-line arrays are sized to this + 1).
17: * Fly to a target and hover there (the ONE "Go to" — user spec). Params: {@code target} = STAR / PLANET /
18: * NEAREST_PLANET / RANDOM_PLANET / RIPPLE / RIPPLE_UNSCANNED / SHIP / HOME (see {@link USSProgramDefaults})
19: * plus an optional {@code index} for PLANET / RIPPLE / SHIP.

24: * {@link USSWorkKind#MINE}, mined at the ship's mining power).
27: * WRITE: write a string into the USS variable space. Params: {@code value} (string), {@code slot} (0..255).
29: * READ: copy one USS variable slot into another. Params: {@code from} (0..255), {@code to} (0..255).
31: * WAIT: hold for a number of ticks. Params: {@code ticks}.
33: * STOP: terminate the program (the ship HOLDS — no implicit return, user decision).

37: * carrying a Voidbase blueprint + parts loadout reaches its target and builds/fills the construction site
38: * there. Params: {@code target} (the anchor body — STAR / PLANET / RIPPLE, with {@code index} for PLANET /
39: * RIPPLE). On a ship this is the CONSTRUCT leg of the Constructor mission; a completed site spawns the base.
43: * REPAIR: restore the station's integrity over time, drawing its own energy buffer (repair work command).
44: * A Voidbase runs this at its anchor (or a Voidcraft at any hover point) to top up the integrity time limit.
45: * Requires a {@link tectech.voidcraft.ship.VoidcraftCoverComponent#REPAIR_BAY} cover; each second of repair
46: * restores one integrity at the bay's energy draw. Params: none.

51: * kind {@link USSWorkKind#SCAN}, scanned at the ship's scan power; the yield is the ripple reveal, not cargo).
55: * SIPHON the star until the work leg completes (the Starlifter's work command — a work leg of kind
56: * {@link USSWorkKind#SIPHON}, siphoned at the ship's starlifter power; the yield is the star cargo).
63: * The block label a command id renders as in the program view ("MOVE" / "MINE" / …); {@code CMD<id>} for an
64: * unknown id (the view's fallback, so a new command is never an empty block).
==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSCommandConstruct.java ====
6: * The CONSTRUCT command (Voidbase construction framework) - build a Voidbase at the current hover point. A
7: * constructor carrying a Voidbase blueprint + parts loadout reaches its target and builds or fills the
8: * construction site there (the first constructor creates it, later ones fill it; a completed site spawns the
9: * base). TIMED: begin arms the site's part-transfer leg (one part per second per 100 construction power) and the
10: * command runs RUNNING - the executor polls {@link #tick} every machine tick until the leg completes, the site
11: * fills, or the leg counts down (leftover parts then stay on board). Unresolvable hover / no blueprint / an
12: * occupied anchor -&gt; FAILED -&gt; the executor SKIPS the instruction.
13: *
14: * <p>
15: * Params: none - the ship hover anchor (the preceding MOVE) defines where the base is built.
==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSCommandHandler.java ====
6: * A Voidcraft command handler (programming framework, Phase B — the modularity seam).
7: *
8: * <p>
9: * A handler is a STATELESS singleton: per-ship, per-instruction state lives in the {@code state} compound the
10: * executor hands in (it is persisted with the executor's cursor, so a handler survives save/reload).
11: *
12: * <ul>
13: * <li>{@link #begin} — the node's first step. Return {@link USSCommandStatus#RUNNING} for long-running commands
14: * (MOVE / WORK / WAIT) — the executor then polls {@link #tick} every tick until it reports otherwise — or
15: * DONE / FAILED / STOP for immediate ones (WRITE / READ / STOP).</li>
16: * <li>{@link #tick} — called every tick while the command is in flight. MOVE/WORK poll {@link
17: * USSExecutionContext#legComplete()} (the legs tick in real time on the game side); WAIT counts down.</li>
18: * </ul>
19: *
20: * <p>
21: * Adding a command = one new handler class + {@link USSCommandRegistry#register} + an id in
22: * {@link USSCommand}. The executor and the program format never change.
26: * @return the command id this handler serves (see {@link USSCommand})
30: * @param ctx   the execution context (the game seam)
31: * @param node  the COMMAND node being executed (read its params here)
32: * @param state this instruction's private state (persisted with the executor; initially empty, reusable
33: *              across save/reload)
34: * @return RUNNING (in flight — the executor polls tick) / DONE / FAILED (skip) / STOP (terminate program)
39: * @param ctx   the execution context
40: * @param node  the COMMAND node still being executed
41: * @param state this instruction's private state (as persisted)
42: * @return RUNNING (keep polling) / DONE / FAILED / STOP

7: * completes (a work leg of kind {@link USSWorkKind#MINE}, mined at the ship's mining power). The work leg runs in
8: * real time on the game side (the shared leg-duration tables); the executor only polls.
9: *
10: * <p>
11: * Params: none (the ship's current target defines the work — set by the preceding MOVE). The game side refuses
12: * the leg when the ship has no mining power (the command then SKIPs).

6: * The WAIT command (programming framework, Phase B) — hold for a number of ticks (the ship keeps doing what it
7: * is doing; legs are unaffected).

12: * quantized to the node-step pace).

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSCondition.java ====
6: * One binary condition of a Voidcraft program (programming framework, Phase A): {@code left OP right} over
7: * {@link USSValue}s.
8: *
9: * <p>
10: * This class is the CONDITION DATA plus the pure comparison of already-resolved strings. Resolving a
11: * {@link USSValue} against the ship's stats and the USS variable space is the executor context's job (Phase B) —
12: * keeping it out here is what makes this bare-JVM testable.
13: *
14: * <p>
15: * Immutable, NBT round-trip, null-safe (corrupt NBT → a safe default condition), no backwards-compat.
29: * Null-safe factory: nulls fall back to the empty-literal / EQ defaults.
50: * Pure comparison of two ALREADY-RESOLVED string values:
51: * <ul>
52: * <li>EQ / NEQ: exact string comparison (case-sensitive); null sides count as {@code ""}.</li>
53: * <li>LT / GT: numeric comparison (trimmed {@code Double.parseDouble}); if either side is not a number the
54: * condition is false.</li>
55: * </ul>
77: not numeric → neither LT nor GT holds
90: region NBT
101: * @param nbt the compound as written by {@link #writeToNBT()} (may be null)
102: * @return the condition — NEVER null; corrupt NBT falls back to {@code "" EQ ""}
112: endregion
==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSConditionOp.java ====
4: * Condition operators of a Voidcraft program (programming framework, Phase A).
5: *
6: * <p>
7: * EQ / NEQ compare strings exactly (case-sensitive). LT / GT compare numerically: both sides are trimmed and
8: * parsed as doubles; if EITHER side is not a number, the condition is false (see
9: * {@link USSCondition#evaluate(String, String)}).
13: * String equality (case-sensitive).
15: * String inequality.
17: * Numeric less-than (unparseable side → false).

103: * its destination and turns right back without mining anything" (user report).

139: * destination 0.5 blocks above that planet"). Pass 9: the rendered planet is a unit CUBE of size spec.scale
140: * (0.175–0.66 = its edge length; its surface sits 0.5·scale above its center), so the renderer adds HALF that
141: * scale on top of this constant — a flat 0.5 over the center would swallow the ship (and the whole laser
142: * beam) inside the planets. The ship tracks the planet's live rendered position while working.

155: * (now 65×65×65), not of the star — the star and planet SIZES are unchanged, only the dome that contains
156: * them doubles. Pass 13 (user: "can be expanded by 1.5 radius while still fitting inside the shell"):

175: * distance · scale / (speed · {@link #SHIP_SPEED_MULTIPLIER}): a 15-block trip at speed 5 takes
176: * {@code 15 · 200 / (5 · 5) = 120} ticks (6 s); a 24-block trip at speed 1 takes 2400 ticks (2 min).

182: * legs complete in a fifth of the normal time). Applied in {@link #travelTicks}, which both the server (leg

189: * divided by speed (the stateful-position pass; supersedes pass 18's fixed {@code 300 / speed}).
190: *
191: * <p>
192: * With {@code speed = thrust / mass} (unclamped, see {@code VoidcraftStats.speedFor}) and {@code distance} the
193: * block-separation between the ship's start and its target, the leg time is
194: * {@code distance · TRAVEL_DISTANCE_SCALE / speed}, clamped to {@link #TRAVEL_TICKS_MIN}–{@link #TRAVEL_TICKS_MAX}
195: * so the creative loop stays fast in both directions and a zero-distance leg is still perceptible.
196: *

6: * <p>

9: * <ul>

15: * {@link USSProgramExecutor#STEP_TICKS} ticks;</li>

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSFleetOrbit.java ====
4: * Phase 4 pass 5: deterministic per-ship "swarm spread" for a USS fleet.
5: *
6: * <p>
7: * With dozens–hundreds of ships (see {@link USSConstants#MAX_SHIPS_PER_USS}), the fleet must not stack at one
8: * point: each ship hovers at a stable, deterministic offset around its role's shared hover point, derived from a
9: * per-ship key — no extra NBT, and the same offset on every client for the life of the world.
10: *
11: * <p>
12: * The key is the USS-assigned <strong>per-launch fleet seed</strong> (see {@link #offsetFor(long)}); the item
13: * UUID is only a fallback. Ships are ITEMS — a player can duplicate one in creative, and every copy shares the
14: * item's {@code vc_uuid}. Keying the spread (and the client's per-ship animation phases) on the item UUID alone
15: * made all duplicates hover at one spot and corrupt each other's leg progress. The seed is assigned once per
16: * launch by the USS and persisted on the ship's mission, so it is unique per flight even for identical items.
17: *
18: * <p>
19: * Bounds: |offset| ≤ {@link #MAX_RADIUS} (2.0 blocks). Pass 13/14 re-based the shell bound: planets orbit from
20: * 3 blocks out to 4 blocks INSIDE the Voidcraft dome (USSPlanets.MAX_DISTANCE = SPACE_SHELL_RADIUS − 4 = 23.1,
21: * dome 27.1 — pass 12's 25.9 + 1.5, −0.3 in pass 15), so the planet center worst case is 0.2 + 23.1 + 0.28 =
22: * 23.58 from the star center — and since the 4-block margin exceeds hover 0.5 + cube half 0.375 + spread 2.0 =
23: * 2.875, even the worst-case ship (≈ 26.46) stays INSIDE the dome (pass 14, user: ships must not exit the dome).
24: * (The legacy
25: * 12.95 dome and the old 12.355 worst case belonged to the pre-pass-13 range; the legacy EoH machine keeps its
26: * 12.95 dome via its own domeRadius default.)
27: *
28: * <p>
29: * Pure function — unit-testable in the bare JVM.
34: * Maximum spread magnitude in blocks (pass 11 removed the bob term). Pass 14: with the 4-block orbit margin
35: * (USSPlanets.MAX_DISTANCE = dome − 4), spread 2.0 + hover 0.5 + planet half ≤ 0.375 = 2.875 < 4.0, so even
36: * the outermost hovering ship stays inside the Voidcraft dome.
40: * Maximum vertical spread component in blocks (before the magnitude clamp below).
44: * Pass 7 — the fleet anchor sits exactly 2 blocks ABOVE the star block (see the USS's anchor layout), so the
45: * star center is at (0, -2, 0) in fleet-anchor coordinates. Shared by the hover math below and the client.
50: * Pass 30 (user: "the orbital speed of planets is not very realistic. Planets at the outer edges move a lot
51: * faster than planets closer to the sun. It should be calculated to roughly approximate real orbital
52: * mechanics, where far out planets move very slowly while closer planets move faster. For pinning the speed to
53: * some numbers, a planet at X blocks should take X minutes to complete one full rotation"): the orbit angle is
54: * a pure function of the orbit RADIUS — inner planets orbit fast, outer planets very slowly (the qualitative
55: * shape of real orbital mechanics). Pass 37 (user: "the planets feel very fast. Make their orbital speed 10x
56: * slower"): the pass-30 pace (a planet at X blocks took X·1200 ticks to orbit, 0.3/X deg per tick) is 10x too
57: * quick, so the constant is 0.03/X deg per tick — a planet at X blocks now takes 10·X minutes (10·X·1200 ticks)
58: * to complete one orbit. The star renderer draws the planets with THIS constant (single source of truth, see
59: * {@code EOHRenderingUtils}), so ships, rendered planets and the server all share one law and never drift
60: * apart.
69: * Pass 7 — the live position of a planet in FLEET-ANCHOR coordinates at the given world time. This is
70: * EXACTLY the orbit math {@code EOHRenderingUtils.renderUSSOrbits} uses to draw the planet (radius
71: * {@code 0.2 + distance + 0.2·starSize}; angle {@code (0.03·time)/radius} — the pass-30 radius law, slowed 10x in
72: * pass 37; tilts
73: * {@code xAngle}/{@code zAngle})
74: * plus the anchor's 2-block offset over the star — so a ship hovering
75: * {@link USSConstants#HOVER_ABOVE_PLANET} above this point sits precisely above the rendered body.
76: *

79: *                   angle is a pure function of the orbit radius (the old random 0.5–1.5 speed made far
80: *                   planets orbit as fast as near ones)

85: * @return {@code double[3]} position in fleet-anchor coordinates (pure function — bare-JVM testable)
90: Pass 30 (user: "a planet at X blocks should take X minutes to complete one full rotation") + pass 37
91: (10x slower): angular speed 0.03/X degrees per tick — inner fast, outer slow. (The old
92: orbitSpeed·0.1·time was independent of radius, so outer planets swept the sky as fast as inner ones.)

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSNode.java ====
13: * One node of a Voidcraft program (programming framework, Phase A).
14: *
15: * <p>
16: * A node is exactly one of:
17: * <ul>
18: * <li>COMMAND — a {@link #cmdId()} plus free-form {@link #params()} (the command's inputs — the modularity seam:
19: * adding a command never changes this format);</li>
20: * <li>IF / WHILE — a {@link #condition()} plus a {@link #body()};</li>
21: * <li>REPEAT — a {@link #count()} plus a {@link #body()}.</li>
22: * </ul>
23: *
24: * <p>
25: * Immutable (params are defensively copied, bodies unmodifiable). NBT round-trip; the read path is
26: * null-safe and structural corruption (unknown type, non-compound body entry, or exceeding the program caps —
27: * see {@link USSProgram}) yields {@code null} so the CALLER drops the whole program: a half-program is worse
28: * than no program (no backwards-compat).
29: *
30: * <p>
31: * Equality covers the program STRUCTURE (type, command id, condition, count, body) — command params are free-form
32: * payload and are deliberately NOT compared (compare them explicitly where it matters, e.g. tests).
43: * REPEAT count ceiling (a sanity bound against NBT garbage — 65535 node steps is already ~30 minutes).
64: * @param cmdId  the command id (see {@link USSCommand}; clamped to ≥ 0)
65: * @param params the command's input params (null → empty compound; defensively copied)
79: * @param condition the guard (null → the default {@code "" EQ ""})
80: * @param body      the body nodes (null → empty; null entries dropped)
87: * @param condition the guard (null → the default {@code "" EQ ""})
88: * @param body      the body nodes (null → empty; null entries dropped)
95: * @param count the iteration count (clamped to 0..{@link #MAX_REPEAT_COUNT}; 0 = the body never runs)
96: * @param body  the body nodes (null → empty; null entries dropped)
156: * The depth of the subtree rooted here (1 = a leaf).
165: * The number of nodes in the subtree rooted here (including this one).
174: region NBT
205: * Read one node (recursively) with the program's caps enforced through {@code used}[0] (the running node
206: * count).
207: *
208: * @param nbt   the node compound (may be null)
209: * @param depth the node's depth (root level = 1)
210: * @param used  one-element array: the running total of nodes read (including this one)
211: * @return the node, or null on structural corruption / unknown type / cap breach (the caller drops the program)
215: a node without a type is structural corruption (getInteger would silently default to 0 =
216: COMMAND)
252: * @return the body nodes, or null on corruption / cap breach (null children are rejected — a body with a hole
253: *         is a broken program). Note: the typed {@code getTagList(key, 10)} accessor is deliberately avoided —
254: *         in 1.7.10 it returns an EMPTY list whenever the stored element type differs from 10, silently hiding
255: *         exactly the corruption we must reject.
261: no body tag → empty body (a legal shape: e.g. WHILE with an empty body)
264: a body that is not a list is structural corruption
268: raw element (1.7.10 NBTTagList has no untyped get(int))
281: endregion

4: * Node types of a Voidcraft program (programming framework, Phase A).
5: *
6: * <p>
7: * COMMAND — a registered command (MOVE / WORK / WRITE / READ / WAIT / STOP — see {@link USSCommand}); IF — a
8: * one-shot conditional body; WHILE — loop while the condition holds; REPEAT — loop the body N times.
9: *
10: * <p>
11: * Deliberately no FOR (user decision: "skip FOR for now — likely to not have any use").
15: * A command: {@code cmdId} + free-form params.
17: * A one-shot conditional body.
19: * A loop that runs while its condition holds.
21: * A loop that runs its body a fixed number of times.

4: * The pilot's game seam (programming framework, Phase C).
5: *
6: * <p>
7: * The {@link USSShipPilot} is bare-JVM (executor + ship + this interface), so it is unit-testable like the
8: * Phase B executor. The game side (the {@code MTEUnstableSolarSystem}) implements this seam: it knows the USS
9: * (variable space, ripple field, ore reserves, star type), the fleet (other ships for {@code SHIP} rendezvous),
10: * and the world (logging, fleet resync, cargo build).
11: *
12: * <p>

14: * / {@code 0} / no-op), never crashes the ship's tick.

43: * Resolve a MOVE target to a destination (the game-side version of

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSPlanetCatalog.java ====
10: * The Voidcraft planet catalog — the 45 rendered planet textures, each registered into {@link USSPlanetRegistry}.
11: *
12: * <p>
13: * Every planet is one of the bundled textures (see {@code assets/tectech/textures/uss/planets/}), sized by its
14: * texture tier ({@link PlanetTier}: tiny 8×, small 12×, normal 16×, big 24×, huge 32×), and either a gas giant or a
15: * rocky world. A planet's hologram scale is the tier's base scale with a ±10% variation (set by
16: * {@link USSPlanetDefinition.Builder#tier(PlanetTier)}); its orbit-ring probability follows the gas-giant rule
17: * (gas giants 50%, normal-and-larger non-giants 10%, tiny/small 0% — see
18: * {@link USSPlanetDefinition#ringChance()}).
19: *
20: * <p>
21: * All planets may orbit any star class; the per-star pool can be refined later (the star-registration reconciliation
22: * pass). The ore sets are placeholders (3 materials each, amount {@value #DEFAULT_ORE_AMOUNT} million, weight
23: * {@value #DEFAULT_ORE_WEIGHT}) to be rebalanced in game design.
24: *
25: * <p>
26: * Call {@link #registerAll()} once (idempotent) to populate the registry. Bare-JVM safe: only
27: * {@link Materials}/{@link USSStarType}/{@link PlanetTier} data.
31: * Placeholder ore amount (in millions) for the catalog — rebalanced later.

37: * Every star class (the catalog is star-agnostic; the per-star pool can be refined later).
48: * Register the 45 catalog planets into the {@link USSPlanetRegistry}. Idempotent — a second call is a no-op.
56: TINY (8× faces) — the smallest rocky bodies.
60: SMALL (12× faces) — small rocky worlds and the two named inner planets.
66: NORMAL (16× faces) — the common mid-tier worlds.
90: BIG (24× faces) — large worlds and the mid gas giants.
107: HUGE (32× faces) — the largest gas giants.

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSPlanetDefinition.java ====
10: * A registered planet: everything that defines a mineable body available around a Voidcraft star.
11: *
12: * <p>
13: * This is the <strong>registration-based</strong> replacement for the old fixed {@link USSPlanetType} enum. Each
14: * planet is defined by exactly five things:
15: * <ol>
16: * <li><b>Texture</b> — the reference drawn as the planet hologram (currently the IORE dimension abbreviation, the
17: * {@code gtneioreplugin.ModBlocks} key of the dimension-display block; see {@link USSPlanetType#getVisual()}).</li>
18: * <li><b>Size range</b> — a {@code [min, max]} pair of floats, each in {@code [0.0, 5.0]} (the rendered cube scale
19: * is drawn from this range).</li>
20: * <li><b>Allowed star types</b> — the {@link USSStarType}s this planet may orbit (a planet may be allowed around
21: * several star classes, unlike the old one-star-per-type pool).</li>
22: * <li><b>Available ores</b> — a list of {@link USSPlanetOre} entries ({@code [ore type, amount (in millions),
23: * weight]}).</li>
24: * <li><b>Available fluids</b> — a list of {@link Materials} that yield a fluid (resolved later via
25: * {@code Materials.get(name).getFluid(mB)} — kept abstract here, never a Forge {@code Fluid}, so this stays
26: * bare-JVM-testable).</li>
27: * </ol>
28: *
29: * <p>
30: * Pure data + validation: no game world, no RNG, no Forge fluid/block objects — unit-testable in a bare JVM. The
31: * registration system (see {@link USSPlanetRegistry}) stores these; the mining mechanism that CONSUMES them is
32: * wired in a later pass.
33: *
34: * <p>
35: * Immutable: use {@link #builder()} to construct; all collections are copied and returned unmodifiable.
39: * Minimum allowed value of the planet size range (user spec: float 0.0–5.0).

69: * The available fluids (materials that yield a fluid; never null; may be empty).

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSPlanetOre.java ====

12: * probability) is interpreted by the mining mechanism, which is deliberately NOT wired in yet — the registration
13: * system only stores the numbers.

35: *                                  {@code weight} is not a positive finite number

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSPlanetRegistry.java ====
11: * The registration-based catalog of Voidcraft planets (the replacement for the old fixed {@link USSPlanetType}
12: * enum).
13: *
14: * <p>
15: * Planets are {@link USSPlanetDefinition}s registered by a stable {@code id}. The registry is a simple global,
16: * insertion-ordered map: register once (see {@link USSPlanetCatalog#registerAll()}), then look up by id, list all,
17: * or take the pool of planets a given {@link USSStarType} may host.
18: *
19: * <p>
20: * Deliberately NOT wired into the mining/voidcraft mechanism yet (that is a later pass) — this is the pure
21: * registration layer. Thread-safe (synchronized); the backing store is a {@link LinkedHashMap} so {@link #all()} is
22: * stable in registration order (important for deterministic generation and tests).
23: *
24: * <p>
25: * Bare-JVM safe: only {@link USSPlanetDefinition}/{@link USSStarType}/{@link gregtech.api.enums.Materials} data —
26: * no Forge fluid/block objects.
37: * Register a planet definition.
38: *
39: * <p>
40: * The definition must carry a non-blank {@code id} (validated by {@link USSPlanetDefinition#builder()}); a
41: * duplicate id is a programming error and rejected.
42: *
43: * @param definition the definition to register (must not be null)
44: * @throws NullPointerException     if {@code definition} is null
45: * @throws IllegalArgumentException if a planet with the same id is already registered
56: * @param id the planet id (null → null)
57: * @return the registered definition, or null when no planet with that id is registered
64: * @return true if a planet with that id is registered
71: * @return all registered planets in registration order (unmodifiable, never null; empty if none registered).
78: * The pool of planets a star type may host — every registered planet that allows that star type, in registration
79: * order.
80: *
81: * @param starType the star type (null → empty list, defensive)
82: * @return the matching planets (unmodifiable, never null; may be empty)
98: * @return the number of registered planets
105: * Remove all registered planets. Intended for tests (and for a future "re-register with different data" flow);
106: * not part of normal runtime use.
==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSPlanets.java ====
13: * Deterministic planet generation for a Voidcraft star (Phase 4 pass 3).
14: *
15: * <p>
16: * A system's planets are a <strong>pure function of (star type, ignition timestamp)</strong>:
17: *
18: * <pre>
19: * planets = USSPlanets.generate(uss.getStarType(), uss.getIgnitedAt())
20: * </pre>
21: *
22: * so the three consumers — the server (what a Miner actually mines), the client (the holograms the legacy EoH
23: * renderer draws) and the GUI (the infodata "Planets" section) — all derive the exact same system with NO extra
24: * NBT to persist and no server→client planet sync beyond what the render TE already carries.
25: *
26: * <p>
27: * <strong>Shape of a system.</strong> A RANDOM planet count — PASS 22 (user: "planet count randomized between
28: * 3-9"): {@code MIN_PLANETS_PER_SYSTEM}…{@code MAX_PLANETS_PER_SYSTEM} (3–9), drawn as the FIRST rng call so
29: * (star type, seed) still yields exactly one fixed system. Types are shuffled WITHOUT replacement from the star
30: * type's pool ({@link USSPlanetType#pool(USSStarType)} — 4 types) and walked in order: the first
31: * {@code min(count, 4)} planets are all different types, and only systems larger than the pool wrap and repeat
32: * (a 9-planet system has at most one type twice). Drawing with replacement (the pass-3 behavior) put duplicate
33: * types in 62.5% of systems — same-colored stacked spheres that read as "one planet" in the infodata. Each planet
34: * gets distinct orbital parameters — pass 13 (user: "the planets are still very close to the star"): distance is
35: * randomized from MIN_DISTANCE (3 blocks from the star) all the way to the system's edge leaving 4 blocks from
36: * the shell edge (pass 14, user: "ships exiting the dome isn't good" — the 2-block margin was smaller than hover
37: * + planet half + spread = 2.875; MAX_DISTANCE = USSConstants.SPACE_SHELL_RADIUS − 4 = 23.1, dome 27.1), clear
38: * of the star surface ≤ 1.9; rendered scale 0.175–0.66 across the texture tiers (the definition's size range
39: * 0.35–1.32 reduced by PLANET_RENDER_SCALE) and orbit/spin speeds 0.5–1.5. The
40: * distance range is split into COUNT bands (inner → outer), so a 9-planet system still spreads across the dome.
41: * Pass 11
42: * (user spec):
43: * all orbits sit on the SAME horizontal (xz) plane like a real solar system, each inclined only 0–5° from it
44: * (the inclination and its direction are decomposed into the renderer's xAngle/zAngle tilt pair, so the
45: * renderer chain and the ship-tracked orbit math are unchanged).
46: *
47: * <p>
48: * No RNG outside the single seeded {@link Random}: the {@code java.util.Random(long)} contract guarantees
49: * identical sequences across JVMs, which is what makes server and client agree.
53: * PASS 22 (user: "planet count randomized between 3-9"): the minimum planets in a system.
56: * PASS 22 (user: "planet count randomized between 3-9"): the maximum planets in a system.
60: * Inner planet distance (blocks from the star center; the star radius is at most 1.9). Pass 13 (user: "planets are
61: * still very close to the star"): no closer than 3 blocks.
66: * Outer planet distance (blocks). Pass 13: the system's edge with a margin from the shell — pass 14 (user:
67: * "the ships being able to exit the dome isn't good — make the distance from the edge 4 blocks"): 4 BLOCKS
68: * of margin, which covers the worst-case ship extension (hover 0.5 + planet half 0.375 + spread 2.0 = 2.875),
69: * so the fleet stays INSIDE the dome. Derived from {@link USSConstants#SPACE_SHELL_RADIUS} (27.1) so the
70: * dome and the orbit range can never drift apart. (Replaces the legacy 4.0–9.0 range inside the 12.95 dome.)
75: * The planet hologram's rendered scale range — the definition's size range (0.35–1.32 across the texture tiers)
76: * reduced by {@link #PLANET_RENDER_SCALE}.
82: * The factor applied to the planet's sampled size for the hologram's rendered scale — planets render half the
83: * size of their definition's size range.
87: * Orbit/spin speed range (the legacy EoH uses 0.5–1.5).
92: * Pass 11 (user spec: "like real solar systems") — each orbit lies on the horizontal (xz) plane inclined by a
93: * small random angle in 0–5° (the legacy ±30° dual tilts are gone; the old constant is replaced).
102: * One generated planet: its registered definition (what can be mined from it + the hologram texture + the size
103: * range) plus the orbital parameters the renderer needs and the sampled scale.
107: * The registered planet definition (carries the mineable ores + fluids, hologram texture, and size range).
110: * Orbit radius in blocks from the star center (within {@link #MIN_DISTANCE}–{@link #MAX_DISTANCE}).
113: * Hologram scale — sampled from the definition's size range (0.0–5.0), applied to the render.
116: * Orbit angular speed factor (within {@link #MIN_SPEED}–{@link #MAX_SPEED}).
119: * Self-rotation speed factor (within {@link #MIN_SPEED}–{@link #MAX_SPEED}).
123: * Orbit-plane tilt about X, in degrees — one component of the small 0–5° inclination (pass 11: coplanar
124: * orbits; |x| ≤ {@link #MAX_INCLINATION_DEG}).
129: * Orbit-plane tilt about Z, in degrees — the other component of the small 0–5° inclination (pass 11:
130: * coplanar orbits; |z| ≤ {@link #MAX_INCLINATION_DEG}).
134: * Whether this planet orbits with a ring (a seeded draw from the definition's ring probability).
138: * The orbit-ring texture variant for this planet, in {@code 1..tier.ringVariantCount()} (normal/big: 1–8,
139: * small/huge: 1–6); {@code -1} when {@link #hasRing} is false (no ring).
196: * Generate the planet system of a star (the registration-based mechanics pass).
197: *
198: * <p>
199: * The system is a pure function of (star type, seed) — the same (starType, seed) always yields the same system,
200: * so server (mining), client (rendering) and GUI (infodata) all derive the identical system. The three inputs
201: * come from the registered definitions:
202: * <ul>
203: * <li><strong>Planet count</strong> — sampled from the star's planet range
204: * ({@link USSStarDefinition#getPlanetMin()}…{@link USSStarDefinition#getPlanetMax()}).</li>
205: * <li><strong>Planet types</strong> — the registered planets that allow this star type
206: * ({@link USSPlanetRegistry#pool(USSStarType)}), shuffled and walked.</li>
207: * <li><strong>Planet size</strong> — sampled from each planet's size range
208: * ({@link USSPlanetDefinition#getSizeMin()}…{@link USSPlanetDefinition#getSizeMax()}, 0.0–5.0).</li>
209: * </ul>
210: *
211: * @param starType the star's type (null → {@link USSStarType#MAIN_SEQUENCE}, defensive) — selects the pool
212: * @param seed     any stable long (the USS ignition timestamp in practice) — same (starType, seed) always yields
213: *                 the same system
214: * @return the system's planets (never null, never empty); planet i orbits in the i-th of COUNT equal distance
215: *         bands {@code MIN_DISTANCE + i*(MAX-MIN)/count … MIN_DISTANCE + (i+1)*(MAX-MIN)/count}
221: The star's planet range (how many planets this star has) — from the registered star definition.
226: The planets that can orbit this star — from the registered planet pool (their allowedStarTypes).
229: Defensive: no registered planets for this star type — fall back to the whole catalog so the system
230: is never empty.
239: The count is the FIRST draw — planetMin…planetMax inclusive — so (starType, seed) still yields exactly
240: one fixed system and server/client agree (Random(long) contract).
243: Distinct types: shuffle the pool with the SAME seeded rng and walk it in order — the first min(count,
244: pool) planets are all different types; only beyond the pool does it wrap.
256: The planet's SIZE — sampled from the definition's size range (0.0–5.0). This is both the hologram
257: scale (the render size of the planet cube) and the size used in the miner's ore-amount calculation
258: (ore.amount × planetSize²).
263: Orbits on the same horizontal xz plane, like real solar systems: the orbit plane is the xz plane
264: inclined by a small random inclination (0–5°) toward a random node direction.
271: Orbit ring: a seeded draw from the definition's ring probability (gas giants 50%, normal+ non-giants 10%,
272: tiny/small 0%). The variant is a uniform pick within the planet's tier ring set (1..count); -1 = no ring.
273: Both draws use the SAME seeded rng, so (starType, seed) yields the identical ring layout on every
274: consumer.
293: * Sample the star's SIZE from its registered size range (0.0–10.0) — a pure function of (star type, seed), so

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSPlanetType.java ====
12: * Fixed catalog of the planet types that orbit a Voidcraft star (Phase 4 pass 3).
13: *
14: * <p>
15: * A planet type is a <em>mineable body</em>: it carries a set of three ore materials that a Miner harvests
16: * (as dust, plus the universal stone dust) when it works a planet of this type — "the different planet types
17: * determine what can be mined from the system". The star's TYPE decides which planet types can exist at all:
18: * every {@link USSStarType} owns a pool of four types and the pools are pairwise disjoint, so a system's
19: * mineable set always stays inside that star's element family — "the star type determines what kind of planets
20: * spawn".
21: *
22: * <p>
23: * <strong>Element families.</strong> Main sequence stars (young, active) host common early elements; white
24: * dwarfs (ancient dense remnants) host dense mid elements; supermassive stars host rare and exotic heavy
25: * elements. All 36 catalog materials are distinct and resolve through the GT material registry.
26: *
27: * <p>
28: * The {@link #visual} field is the IORE dimension abbreviation (the {@code gtneioreplugin.ModBlocks} key) of
29: * the dimension-display block drawn as the planet hologram — the legacy EoH renderer pipeline draws any orbiting
30: * object's block as-is, so reusing these blocks keeps the planet look consistent with the legacy EoH.
31: *
32: * <p>
33: * Pure data: no game world, no RNG — unit-testable in a bare JVM (the materials resolve through
34: * {@link gregtech.api.enums.Materials#getName()} / {@link gregtech.api.enums.Materials#get(String)} only).
38: Main sequence — young star, common elements.
44: White dwarf — ancient dense remnant, dense mid elements.
50: Supermassive — extreme star, rare/exotic heavy elements.
56: * Stable numeric id (0–11, in catalog order).
59: * Lang key for the human-readable name (see the en/ru lang files).
62: * The three ore materials a Miner harvests from a planet of this type (order is display order).
65: * IORE dimension abbreviation — the key of the dimension-display block drawn as the planet hologram.
68: * The star type whose pool this planet type belongs to.
76: Catalog grouping (declaration order): ids 0–3 main sequence, 4–7 white dwarf, 8–11 supermassive.
96: * @return the human-readable lang key of this planet type (e.g. {@code tt.voidcraft_uss.planet.rocky_world}).
103: * @return the three ore materials of this planet type (never null; see class javadoc).
110: * @return the IORE dimension abbreviation of the hologram block (see class javadoc).

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSPosition.java ====
6: * A point in the Voidcraft solar system (the stateful-position pass): the 3D position of a star, planet, or ship.
7: *
8: * <p>
9: * <strong>Coordinate system.</strong> FLEET-ANCHOR coordinates (see {@link USSFleetOrbit}) — the same space the EoH
10: * renderer and the ship-tracked orbit math already use, where the star center sits at {@code (0, STAR_CENTER_Y, 0)}
11: * = {@code (0, -2, 0)}. Everything is in <em>blocks</em>, so the Euclidean distance between two positions is the
12: * physical separation the ships fly across — "the notion of distance within the solar system."
13: *
14: * <p>
15: * <strong>Bare-JVM safe.</strong> Only three {@code double} components — no Forge objects — so the vector math,
16: * the distance model, and the travel-time math (distance / speed) are unit-testable without a world or a live
17: * renderer. NBT read/write is provided for persistence on the ship.
18: *
19: * <p>
20: * The class is IMMUTABLE: every operation returns a new instance, so a ship's stored position is never mutated in
21: * place (the state machine hands out a fresh position each tick).

41: * @param y the Y coordinate (anchor blocks)
42: * @param z the Z coordinate (anchor blocks)

58: *         fixed position of the star; see {@link USSFleetOrbit#STAR_CENTER_Y})
76: region vector math

96: *

108: * @return {@code this + (other - this) · t}

126: * @return {@code (x + dx, y + dy, z + dz)}

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSProgram.java ====
11: * A Voidcraft program: an ordered root list of {@link USSNode}s (programming framework, Phase A).
12: *
13: * <p>
14: * The program is what the CONTROLLER holds (user spec: "the Voidcraft controller itself holds a list of
15: * instructions that is user-editable"). It is digitized into the ship item NBT ({@code vc_program}) and executed
16: * by the per-ship executor (Phase B).
17: *
18: * <p>
19: * Caps (enforced on the NBT READ path — a program that exceeds them is dropped wholesale and yields the empty
20: * program, i.e. corrupt/oversized NBT is treated exactly like corrupt NBT: no partial program, no migration):
21: * at most {@link #MAX_NODES} nodes in total and nesting of at most {@link #MAX_DEPTH}. The in-code path
22: * ({@link #of(List)}) is trusted and does not enforce the caps.
23: *
24: * <p>
25: * Immutable, bare-JVM (NBT + JDK only) — unit-testable (see {@code USSProgramTest}).
29: * Maximum TOTAL number of nodes (all nesting levels).
31: * Maximum nesting depth (a root node is depth 1; its leaf descendants are one more each).
33: * Maximum string-literal length (a program carries short strings, not blobs).
47: * @param nodes the root nodes (null → empty program; null entries are dropped)
74: * The maximum depth of any root node (0 for an empty program).
83: * The total number of nodes across all nesting levels.
92: region NBT
95: * @return the program as an NBT list of node compounds (stored under the ship's {@code vc_program} tag)
106: * @param list the tag list as written by {@link #writeToNBT()} (may be null)
107: * @return the program — NEVER null; a missing/corrupt list, a non-compound entry, an unknown node type, or a
108: *         program over the caps ({@link #MAX_NODES}/{@link #MAX_DEPTH}) yields the EMPTY program
117: raw element (1.7.10 NBTTagList has no untyped get(int))
130: endregion

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSProgramEditor.java ====
13: * The pure program EDITOR (pass-33, programming UI — the bare-JVM core of the Controller's program editor).
14: *
15: * <p>
16: * The editor applies ONE edit to an immutable {@link USSProgram} and returns either the NEW program (accepted) or
17: * a REJECTION with a human-readable reason (shown in the GUI; the program is left untouched). It is the single
18: * place where program shape is validated, so every path to a stored program (GUI actions, preset buttons, future
19: * remote writes) enforces the same rules:
20: * <ul>
21: * <li>caps — {@link USSProgram#MAX_NODES} total nodes, {@link USSProgram#MAX_DEPTH} nesting,
22: * {@link USSProgram#MAX_LITERAL_LENGTH} per literal, {@link USSNode#MAX_REPEAT_COUNT} per REPEAT;</li>
23: * <li>parameter schema — each command's known params, their NBT types (the executor reads
24: * {@code getInteger}/{@code getLong}/{@code getString}), and their ranges (slots 0..255, MOVE targets from the
25: * known set, WAIT ticks ≥ 0);</li>
26: * <li>structural sanity — flow nodes for condition/count edits, bodies for list edits, valid node addresses.</li>
27: * </ul>
28: *
29: * <p>
30: * <b>Path semantics</b> (the shared vocabulary between this class and the GUI):
31: * <ul>
32: * <li>a NODE is addressed by {@code int[] path}: {@code [i]} = the i-th root node; {@code [i, k]} = the k-th node
33: * in the body of the i-th root node; {@code [i, k, m]} = one level deeper, and so on (indices 0-based);</li>
34: * <li>a LIST is addressed by the ADDRESS OF ITS OWNER: an empty {@code []} = the root list;
35: * {@code [i]} = the body of the i-th root node; {@code [i, k]} = the body of node {@code [i, k]}</li>
36: * <li>every edit is a pure function: the input program is never mutated, the result is a new {@link USSProgram}.</li>
37: * </ul>
38: *
39: * <p>
40: * Bare-JVM (NBT + JDK only) — unit-testable (see {@code USSProgramEditorTest}).
44: region Result
47: * The outcome of one edit: either the accepted NEW program ({@link #program()}) or a rejection
48: * ({@link #error()} — a short, user-visible reason).

53: null when accepted
68: * @return true when the edit was applied ({@link #program()} holds the new program)

84: endregion
86: region MOVE target whitelist (the editor's schema knows the full target set — including pass-33's RANDOM_PLANET)
104: endregion

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSProgramExecutor.java ====
12: * The per-ship program EXECUTOR (programming framework, Phase B) — runs a {@link USSProgram} over the ship's
13: * lifetime.
14: *
15: * <p>
16: * Contract (user decisions):
17: * <ul>
18: * <li><b>Pacing</b> — one node step per second: {@link #STEP_TICKS} (20) ticks per node transition (decision #6,
19: * "support much larger amount of in-flight Voidcraft"). Long-running commands (MOVE / WORK / WAIT) run in REAL
20: * TIME meanwhile — their handlers are polled every tick; the legs themselves tick on the game side. A
21: * {@code WHILE(true){…}} body therefore takes at least 20 ticks per node, so a busy-loop can never consume a
22: * tick budget.</li>
23: * <li><b>Failure → SKIP</b> (decision #3) — a FAILED instruction (unresolvable MOVE target, missing params,
24: * unknown command id, refused leg) is logged and the program continues with the NEXT node. There is no HALT
25: * state.</li>
26: * <li><b>Invisible while — the program repeats</b> — when the root scope exhausts the executor restarts at the
27: * FIRST node (an invisible {@code while(true)} around the whole program): a one-command program (a lone WORK)
28: * runs forever. The program ENDS — the executor becomes {@link State#COMPLETED} — only on a STOP, an empty
29: * program, or a corrupt-cursor fail-safe; at the end nothing else happens: no implicit MOVE HOME, no delivery,
30: * no re-emission.</li>
31: * </ul>
32: *
33: * <p>
34: * <b>Resumable</b>: the whole cursor (scope stack + active instruction + step timer) serializes to NBT, so a ship
35: * mid-while-loop survives save/reload (the game pilot stores this under {@code vc_exec}).
36: *
37: * <p>
38: * <b>Control-flow model</b>: a stack of {@link Scope}s. The root scope holds the program's nodes; an IF / WHILE /
39: * REPEAT pushes its body as a new scope WITHOUT advancing past itself — when the body scope exhausts, the executor
40: * resolves the owning control node (IF → advance past it; WHILE → re-evaluate, re-loop or advance; REPEAT →
41: * counter: next iteration or advance past it). Exactly one node transition per step.
45: * Ticks per node step (decision #6 — one node step per second).
48: * The executor's state.
51: * The program is running (instructions still pending, or an instruction in flight).
53: * The program ended (a STOP ran, or the program was empty) — the ship/base HOLDS.
74: * The kinds of scopes in the cursor (the root + the bodies of IF / WHILE / REPEAT).
100: * One level of the cursor: a node list + where we are inside it (+ the REPEAT iteration counter).
129: * The active in-flight instruction (MOVE / WORK / WAIT) + its private handler state.
169: * Start executing a program (fresh cursor at the first node). An empty / null program completes immediately
170: * (nothing to do — the ship then holds).
171: *
172: * @param program the program (null-safe)
173: * @return a RUNNING executor, or a COMPLETED one when there is nothing to run
188: * One tick. Call every tick while the ship exists (the game pilot does; the cost is a countdown decrement
189: * plus — only while an instruction is in flight — one handler poll).
190: *
191: * @param ctx the execution context (null → no-op)
208: still in flight — keep polling next tick
219: restart the rhythm on the next node step
230: * One node transition (called on the 20-tick boundary).
245: * Resolve an exhausted scope (one scope transition per step): pop it, then resolve the control node that owns
246: * its body (IF → advance past; WHILE → re-evaluate; REPEAT → counter).
252: nothing to repeat — the program ends
254: invisible while: the program starts over
262: body done — continue after the IF
267: another iteration
269: false: the parent stays AT the WHILE — the next step re-evaluates and advances
275: next iteration
277: count exhausted — continue after the REPEAT
281: defensive — a body always belongs to IF/WHILE/REPEAT
286: * Process one node: exactly one transition, always.
302: empty body → exhausts on the next step → re-evaluation: at least 20 ticks per iteration
309: count 0 — the body never runs (Phase A contract)
311: counter starts at 1 — the first iteration begins

331: in flight — tick() polls from here on
337: STOP — the only way a non-empty program ends
340: DONE — next node
345: the parent stays AT the owner — handleExhausted resolves it when the body finishes
365: * Advance the top scope past its current node (with the root-exhaustion → COMPLETED check).
378: * The invisible-while check: when the ROOT scope exhausts the program restarts at its first node (a lone
379: * command loops forever) — only a STOP / an empty program ends it.
385: nothing to repeat — the program ends
387: restart the program
393: * @return the condition's verdict, with both sides resolved through the context (null-safe: a missing side
394: *         reads as {@code ""})

419: * @return true while an instruction is in flight (MOVE / WORK / WAIT)
425: * Serialize the cursor (scopes + active instruction + step timer). The program itself is stored separately
426: * (the game pilot keeps both); the cursor is self-contained (each scope carries its body), so
427: * {@link #readFromNBT} never needs the program.
445: * Restore a cursor (save/reload mid-program). Corrupt input is fail-safe: any unreadable scope or active
446: * record drops the whole cursor into COMPLETED (the ship holds — never a half-run, never a double-run from
447: * the start).

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSProgramSync.java ====
16: * The ONE server-side entry for program edits from the Controller GUI (pass 33 UI, UI-2). Parses one ACTION
17: * JSON and applies it with {@link USSProgramEditor}; the result carries the new program (accepted) or a
18: * user-visible rejection reason. Pure — bare JVM testable (see {@code USSProgramSyncTest}).
19: *
20: * <p>
21: * Action JSON (everything the GUI sends is text):
22: * <ul>
23: * <li>{@code {"op":"insert","path":[0],"index":1,"node":{"t":0,"c":0,"p":{"target":"HOME"}}}};</li>
24: * <li>{@code {"op":"remove","path":[0]}};</li>
25: * <li>{@code {"op":"move","path":[0],"up":true}};</li>
26: * <li>{@code {"op":"copy","path":[0]}} (duplicate the block right after itself);</li>
27: * <li>{@code {"op":"param","path":[0],"key":"target","value":"STAR"}} (literal) or
28: * {@code {"op":"param","path":[0],"key":"value","var":17}} (assign a USS slot reference to a WRITE value);</li>
29: * <li>{@code {"op":"count","path":[0],"value":5}};</li>
30: * <li>{@code {"op":"cond","path":[0],"side":0,"lit":"x"}} | {@code …,"var":3} | {@code …,"stat":5};</li>
31: * <li>{@code {"op":"condop","path":[0],"operator":"EQ"}} (EQ / NEQ / LT / GT);</li>
32: * <li>{@code {"op":"apply","preset":"miner"|"starlifter"|"explorer"|"constructor"|"clear"}}.</li>
33: * </ul>
34: *
35: * Node spec (the insert {@code node}): {@code {"t":0,"c":<cmdId>,"p":{…}}} |
36: * {@code {"t":1|"t":2,"l":<value>,"op":<0-3>,"r":<value>,"b":[…]}} | {@code {"t":3,"n":N,"b":[…]}}.
37: * Value spec: {@code {"k":0,"s":"lit"}} | {@code {"k":1,"v":17}} | {@code {"k":2,"st":5}}.
41: * The outcome of one action: the resulting program (accepted) or a user-visible rejection reason.
66: * Apply one action JSON to {@code current}. NEVER mutates {@code current}; never throws (bad JSON →
67: * rejection).
74: * Apply one action JSON to {@code current} with the craft's CAPABILITY SET (the capability system): inserts
75: * and copies of a command the craft cannot run, and preset applications the craft lacks, are rejected (the
76: * rejection message is user-visible in the GUI).
77: *
78: * @param current    the program being edited (null = empty)
79: * @param actionJson the action JSON (see the class javadoc)
80: * @param caps       the craft's capability set (null = no capability check)
164: NOTE: the operator field is "operator" — "op" is the action discriminator (Gson keeps the
165: LAST value for a duplicated key, so the two would collide).
214: region node / value specs
217: * Node spec → node (null when malformed). Bodies are optional (default empty).
301: * Value spec → value (null when malformed): {@code {"k":0,"s":…}} literal | {@code {"k":1,"v":slot}} VAR |
302: * {@code {"k":2,"st":id}} STAT.

19: * NOT here — a point never needs rescanning once revealed.

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSRipplePoint.java ====

9: * evenly-spaced sphere shells (see {@link USSRipples}) — and is either a RIPPLE or not. A point starts HIDDEN

11: * ripple. Scanned ripple points render as a pulsating dark-blue transparent triangle (client side).

28: * The point's Z cell of the 7×7×7 grid (0..6).

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSRipples.java ====
8: * Spacetime-ripple field generation (the Explorer mechanic).
9: *
10: * <p>
11: * Every solar system carries a uniformly spaced {@link #GRID_SIZE}×{@link #GRID_SIZE}×{@link #GRID_SIZE} point grid
12: * (343 points). The field is a PURE function of (star type, seed) — the same inputs always yield the identical
13: * field — so server, client, and GUI agree. The ripple COUNT is sampled inclusively from the star's
14: * {@code spacetimeRipples} range ({@link USSStarDefinition#getRippleMin()}…{@link USSStarDefinition#getRippleMax()},
15: * 0–128), and the ripple POINTS are chosen deterministically (seeded).
16: *
17: * <p>
18: * <strong>Shell mapping.</strong> "Visually, the spots should be mapped to 3 different sphere shells with uniform
19: * spacing." Each grid point is assigned to one of {@link #NUM_SHELLS} shells by its radius from the grid center
20: * (three equal radial bands), and then placed on that shell's sphere — the shells sit at evenly-spaced radii
21: * ({@link #shellRadius(int)}: {@code RIPPLE_SHELL_RADIUS/3, 2·/3, RIPPLE_SHELL_RADIUS}), all relative to the star
22: * center ({@link USSPosition#starCenter()}), so every spot is a real position within the solar system.
23: *
24: * <p>
25: * Bare-JVM safe + deterministic (a {@link Random} seeded by {@code seed} and a fixed salt — the
26: * {@code java.util.Random(long)}
27: * contract guarantees identical sequences on server and client).
28: *
29: * @see USSRippleField
30: * @see USSRipplePoint
34: * The grid edge length: a 7×7×7 field (user spec).
36: * The number of visual shells the grid is mapped onto (user spec: 3).
38: * Total points in the field (7³ = 343).
41: * The OUTERMOST shell radius in blocks (the inner shells are at ⅓ and ⅔ of this, evenly spaced). Chosen to sit
42: * comfortably inside the Voidcraft dome ({@code USSConstants.SPACE_SHELL_RADIUS} = 27.1) and across the planet
43: * orbit band (3..23.1).
47: * The star center in fleet-anchor coordinates — the origin of every ripple position.
50: * The maximum grid radius (a corner of the 7×7×7 cube, 3 from the center on each axis).
53: * Seed salt (the ASCII "USSRIPPE") so the field draws are independent of the planet/star size salts.
61: * The render radius (in blocks, relative to the star center) of the given shell: the shells are at
62: * {@code RIPPLE_SHELL_RADIUS/3, 2·RIPPLE_SHELL_RADIUS/3, RIPPLE_SHELL_RADIUS} (evenly spaced).
63: *
64: * @param shell a shell index (0 = innermost … {@code NUM_SHELLS-1} = outermost)
65: * @return the shell radius in blocks (always &gt; 0 for a valid shell)
73: * Generate a star system's spacetime-ripple field (the Explorer mechanic).
74: *
75: * <p>
76: * A pure function of (star type, seed): the ripple COUNT is sampled inclusively from the star's
77: * {@code spacetimeRipples} range, and the ripple POINTS are chosen by a seeded shuffle of the 343 grid indices —
78: * so (starType, seed) yields exactly ONE fixed field and server/client agree.
79: *
80: * @param starType the star's type (null → {@link USSStarType#MAIN_SEQUENCE}, defensive)
81: * @param seed     any stable long (the USS ignition timestamp in practice)
82: * @return the field (never null; always the full 343-point grid, with {@code rippleCount} ripples)
93: The ripple count is the FIRST draw — rippleMin…rippleMax inclusive (Random(long) contract → deterministic).
96: Build the full 343-point grid (positions are pure — no RNG).
106: Assign to a shell by the radial band (three equal bands), then place on that shell's sphere.
109: The grid center (r=0) has no direction — give it the +Y axis so it still lands on a shell.
120: Choose the ripple points: a seeded shuffle of the 343 indices, take the first `rippleCount`.
132: Mark the chosen points as ripples.

15: * Deterministic cargo of a completed mission (plan Phase 3; Phase 4 pass 1 adds Starlifter cargo; Phase 4 pass 3
16: * switches the miner to the star's planets):
17: *
18: * <p>
19: * <strong>Miner</strong> (Phase 4 pass 3): the miner works the star's <em>planets</em> — cargo is the union
20: * of the planets' ore materials (deduplicated, {@link USSPlanets#materialsOf(List)}) plus stone dust, all as dust.
21: * The system's planets come from the star's TYPE (its planet pool — {@link USSPlanetType}) — "the different
22: * planet types determine what can be mined from the system".
23: *
24: * <p>
25: * <strong>Abstract representation.</strong> While cargo lives on the ship (and in its NBT) it is a list of
26: * <em>abstract entries</em> — {@code {id: item id, Damage: meta, amount: int}} — NOT {@link ItemStack}s. Amounts are
27: * plain ints (the 1.7.10 NBT {@code Count} byte and GT's 64-item stack cap do not apply), so a mission can carry
28: * 10 000 dust without ever materializing 157 stacks. The conversion to 64-chunked {@link ItemStack}s happens exactly
29: * once, at the delivery boundary ({@link #toStacks(NBTTagList)}, called by the bay's
30: * {@code deliver} before the pool merge — the pool is the only 64-slot structure involved).
31: *
32: * <p>

34: * {@link USSConstants#minerStoneDustAmount(long)} (stone dust).

96: * decremented by the mined amount — so ores deplete over the planet's lifetime.

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSShipPilot.java ====
11: * The per-ship PILOT (programming framework, Phase C) — the brain of a Voidcraft in flight.
12: *
13: * <p>
14: * The pilot owns one ship's program: it runs the {@link USSProgramExecutor} (Phase B) against the ship, and it
15: * implements the executor's {@link USSExecutionContext} game seam by bridging to the {@link USSPilotWorld} (the
16: * MTE: USS variable space, ripple field, fleet, cargo, logging). Bare-JVM throughout (NBT + primitives + these
17: * seams), so the full program-driven mission loop is unit-testable with a fake world
18: * (see {@code USSShipPilotTest}).
19: *
20: * <p>
21: * Tick model (one {@link #tick()} per game tick, called by the MTE's fleet loop):
22: * <ol>
23: * <li>the ship's active leg counts down (legs tick in REAL time — the executor's 20-tick pacing never distorts a
24: * leg);</li>
25: * <li>a leg that just completed is consumed EXACTLY ONCE (its side-effect — a WORK leg's cargo/reveal, or a
26: * HOME leg's delivery — fires here, then the completion is handed to the executor as
27: * {@link #legComplete()} on the same tick);</li>
28: * <li>the executor steps (it only transitions a node on its 20-tick boundary);</li>
29: * <li>when the program ends (a STOP ran — or the program was empty) the ship HOLDS
30: * ({@link USSShipState#HOVERING}) — no implicit return (user decision); a finished program wraps and runs
31: * again (the executor's invisible while), so only STOP ends it.</li>
32: * </ol>
33: *
34: * <p>
35: * Persistence: the pilot's state (the executor's cursor + the in-flight leg's bookkeeping + the last resolved
36: * target) serializes into the SHIP's NBT under {@code vc_pilot} (the MTE composes it — see
37: * {@link MTEUnstableSolarSystem}), so a ship saved mid-leg / mid-loop resumes EXACTLY where it stopped: the
38: * side-effect fires exactly once across a reload (the leg-complete latch is persisted with the leg).
42: * The tag the MTE nests the pilot under in the ship's NBT.
54: * The ship's deterministic RNG (re-seeded on attach from the ship's per-launch seed).
58: * The ship's launch origin (fleet-anchor coordinates) — the HOME destination.
61: region leg bookkeeping (persisted — the leg's side-effect must fire exactly once across a reload)
63: * The leg in flight (or the last one)'s work kind (see {@link USSWorkKind}; TRAVEL = a travel leg).
65: * The leg in flight (or the last one) was the HOME leg (MOVE HOME — delivery on completion).
68: * A {@code MOVE HOME} was resolved but its leg not started yet (consumed by the next {@link #startLeg}).
69: * Runtime only (never persists in a meaningful state).
73: * A leg completed and the executor has not observed it yet (runtime only — set when the leg completes, read
74: * by the executor's {@link #legComplete()} on the same tick, reset by the next leg start / hold).
78: endregion
80: * The last resolved MOVE target kind (the MOVE {@code target} string) — the WORK leg's side-effect key.
82: * The last resolved MOVE target's body index (planet i / ripple i / -1 star / -2 ship).
94: region lifecycle
