==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSShipState.java ====
4: * Flight states of a Voidcraft while it is out in the Unstable Solar System.
5: *
6: * <p>
7: * Programming framework (Phase C): the states no longer form a fixed OUTBOUND → MINING → RETURNING loop — the
8: * ship's PROGRAM decides its legs. OUTBOUND = a travel leg toward a body, MINING = a work leg at a body,
9: * RETURNING = the travel leg home ({@code MOVE HOME}) whose completion delivers the ship, HOVERING = holding in
10: * place (program finished without a return — the ship stays). The ship's INTEGRITY is its time limit: it starts at
11: * the ship's maximum when it enters the USS, drops by 1 per second while it is in the USS, and at 0 the ship is
12: * LOST (removed, cargo discarded). A ship that finishes before the limit expires survives — its item reappears at
13: * the gateway, with its integrity back at maximum. The state is persisted as a stable id in NBT (see
14: * {@link VoidcraftActiveShip}).
21: * Docked in the gateway (render-only state — the item is in the gateway's ship slot, not in flight).
24: * Holding in place (programming framework, Phase C): the ship's program finished (or was stopped) and the
25: * ship is NOT returning — it HOLDS at its current position (user decision: no implicit MOVE HOME at program
26: * end; "programming the ship is part of the challenge"). The client renders it hovering at its last body.
47: * @param id persisted state id.
48: * @return the matching state, or null when the id is unknown (corrupt NBT).

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSStarDefinition.java ====
8: * A registered star: the definition of a star type in a Voidcraft system (the registration-based replacement for the
9: * fixed {@link USSStarType} enum as the definition source).
10: *
11: * <p>
12: * Each star is defined by an internal {@code id} (the unique registration key) plus these fields:
13: * <ol>
14: * <li><b>Name method</b> — a string-returning function ({@link Supplier}) that produces the star's name (so names can
15: * be generated procedurally per instance rather than fixed).</li>
16: * <li><b>Type</b> — the <em>display</em> type name (e.g. "Supermassive", "White Dwarf"). Distinct from the
17: * {@code id}.</li>
18: * <li><b>Size range</b> — a {@code [min, max]} pair of floats, each in {@code [0.0, 10.0]}.</li>
19: * <li><b>Main / secondary / tertiary materials</b> — three {@link USSStarMaterial} slots (material + weight, no
20: * amount), primarily fluids.</li>
21: * <li><b>Luminosity</b> — a float in {@code [0.0, 10.0]}.</li>
22: * <li><b>Planet range</b> — a {@code [min, max]} pair of ints, each in {@code [0, 16]} (how many planets the star
23: * hosts).</li>
24: * <li><b>Texture</b> — the texture reference (a string key for now).</li>
25: * <li><b>Evolution target</b> — the {@code id} of the star this one evolves into, or null when it is a terminal
26: * type.</li>
27: * <li><b>Spacetime ripples</b> — a {@code [min, max]} pair of ints, each in {@code [0, 128]}: the range the system's
28: * spacetime-ripple count is sampled from at creation (see {@link USSRipples}; the Explorer ships' target).</li>
29: * <li><b>Color</b> — an opaque ARGB int: the color the star's render mesh is tinted with (what visually
30: * distinguishes the star classes — e.g. main sequence yellow, white dwarf white, supermassive blue).</li>
31: * </ol>
32: *
33: * <p>
34: * Pure data + validation: no game world, no RNG, no Forge fluid/block objects — unit-testable in a bare JVM. The
35: * registration system (see {@link USSStarRegistry}) stores these; the mechanics that CONSUME them (star evolution,
36: * luminosity scaling, planet-count drawing) are wired in a later pass.
37: *
38: * <p>
39: * Immutable: use {@link #builder()} to construct.

17: * resolve a star's evolution target.

21: * registration layer. Thread-safe (synchronized); the backing store is a {@link LinkedHashMap} so {@link #all()} is
22: * stable in registration order (important for deterministic generation and tests).

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSTarget.java ====
6: * What a Voidcraft is flying toward (the stateful-position pass): a star, a planet, or ANOTHER ship.
7: *
8: * <p>
9: * "Voidcraft should be able to fly to any of the three [star, planets, voidcraft]. Voidcraft hold a target, which
10: * determines how they are moving." The target is the ship's destination:
11: * <ul>
12: * <li>{@link Kind#STAR} — the star center (Starlifters' natural work point).</li>
13: * <li>{@link Kind#PLANET} — a system planet (index into the system's planet list); a Miner hovers at a random
14: * spherical-shell point around it (see {@link USSFleetOrbit#shellPoint}).</li>
15: * <li>{@link Kind#SHIP} — another ship in the fleet (identified by its per-launch seed); the targeting ship
16: * nudges itself to hover near it without overlapping (see {@link USSFleetOrbit#nudge}).</li>
17: * </ul>
18: *
19: * <p>
20: * Bare-JVM safe (an enum + two ints) and NBT-serializable so it persists on the ship. The KIND is the routing
21: * discriminator; the two payload ints are only meaningful for their matching kind (the other is ignored, defaulted
22: * to 0) so the NBT shape stays flat.

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\USSTargetResult.java ====
4: * The result of resolving a MOVE target (programming framework, Phase C): the concrete DESTINATION point in the
5: * solar system plus the BODY DESCRIPTOR the client hovers at (a planet index, a ripple-point index, the star, or
6: * a rendezvous point).
7: *
8: * <p>

10: * <ul>
11: * <li>{@code i ≥ 0} — planet index or ripple-point index (the client resolves it against the fleet TE's system,
12: * exactly as it already does for a mission target);</li>
13: * <li>{@code -1} — the star (the client hovers above the star center);</li>
14: * <li>{@code -2} — a ship rendezvous (no system body — the client hovers at the resolved {@code position});</li>
15: * <li>{@code -3} — HOME (the launch origin — the client treats this as a travel leg to the gateway).</li>
16: * </ul>
17: *
18: * <p>
19: * {@code staticBody}: true when the hover point is a FIXED position (ripple points, ship rendezvous) and the
20: * client must hover at {@code position} exactly; false when the client should track the body's LIVE position
21: * (planets keep orbiting — the ship follows the planet, as it already does during a work leg).

48: * @return the body descriptor (see the class javadoc conventions)
53: * @return true when the client hovers at the fixed {@code position} (not the body's live position)

21: * It lives on the USS (Phase C wiring) so ships, external machines, and the (next-pass) UI all share one space.

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\VoidcraftActiveShip.java ====
9: * A Voidcraft in flight inside an Unstable Solar System: a PASSIVE leg driver (no mission state machine — the
10: * ship's PILOT decides its legs, programming framework Phase C).
11: *
12: * <p>
13: * The ship knows ONE thing: its current leg. A leg has a state ({@link USSShipState#OUTBOUND} a travel leg to a
14: * body, {@link USSShipState#MINING} a work leg, {@link USSShipState#RETURNING} the travel leg home), a start
15: * point, an endpoint, a duration, and a countdown. {@link #startLeg} arms it, {@link #tickLeg} counts it down,
16: * {@link #isLegComplete()} latches completion (consumed exactly once via {@link #clearLegComplete()} by the
17: * pilot — its side-effect fires exactly once, even across a save/reload), and {@link #hold()} parks the ship
18: * ({@link USSShipState#HOVERING} — the program finished without a return; user decision: no implicit MOVE HOME).
19: *
20: * <p>
21: * Bare-JVM (NBT + primitives only) so the ship + pilot stay unit-testable without Forge. Cargo is built externally
22: * (see {@link USSShipCargo}) and handed in via {@link #setCargo(NBTTagCompound)} on the WORK leg's completion —
23: * this class stays free of {@code Materials} so tests run without a live ore dictionary.
24: *
25: * <p>
26: * The ship's INTEGRITY is its TIME LIMIT: it is set to the ship's maximum (the blueprint's integrity) when the
27: * ship enters the USS, drops by 1 per second while the ship is in the USS ({@link #tickIntegrity()}), and when
28: * it reaches 0 the ship is LOST (removed from the USS, its cargo discarded — no delivery, no re-emission). A
29: * ship that finishes its program before the limit expires survives: its item is re-emitted into the gateway's
30: * output bus (dropped at the USS when the bus cannot absorb it), with its integrity back at maximum for the
31: * next flight.
64: * Pass 27 (user: "the cargo hold size should be increased by a factor of 100 — as currently the mining is
65: * basically instant"): the hold capacity is the blueprint's {@code cargoSlots} times this. 100× makes a trip
66: * carry a real haul instead of filling (and thus completing) near-instantly. Applied at the single capacity
67: * source ({@link #cargoCapacity()}) so the hold, its NBT, and the derived delivery cargo all scale together.
72: * The integrity time limit drops by 1 every {@code TICKS_PER_INTEGRITY} ticks — 20 = ONE game second, so the
73: * ship's integrity is exactly its survival budget in SECONDS while it is in the USS.
85: * Per-launch identity (pass 5.1): assigned by the USS at launch, unique per flight even for DUPLICATED ship
86: * items (creative copies share the item's {@code vc_uuid}). The client keys each ship's animation phase and
87: * its swarm-spread spot ({@code USSFleetOrbit}) on this — duplicated items must not share one spot or one
88: * leg-progress counter. 0 = unset (legacy save; the client falls back to the item UUID).
93: * The body descriptor the CLIENT hovers at (programming framework, Phase C): a system planet index, a
94: * ripple-point index, or {@code -1} for the star (no body yet — a fresh ship at the origin). Set by the pilot
95: * when a MOVE target resolves (the ship's hover body for the leg and the work leg that follows).
97: * The hover body descriptor (the pilot sets it when a MOVE target resolves). -1 = star / none.
101: * True when the hover body is a FIXED point (ripple / ship rendezvous) — the client hovers at the resolved
102: * destination.
109: * The ship's INTEGRITY (the time-limit pass): its survival budget while in the USS. Set to the ship's maximum
110: * (the blueprint's integrity, from the payload) when the ship enters the USS, then drops by 1 per second
111: * ({@link #tickIntegrity()}). At 0 the ship is lost (removed, cargo discarded).
115: * Ticks until the next integrity drop (re-armed to {@link #TICKS_PER_INTEGRITY} after each drop).
118: region the current leg (a passive countdown — the pilot arms + consumes it)
123: * Latched the moment the countdown hits zero — consumed exactly once via {@link #clearLegComplete()}.
126: * The current leg's start point (the ship's position when the leg began — the client lerps from it on a
127: * travel leg).
130: * The current leg's endpoint (the point the ship flies to / works at).
132: * The current leg's distance in blocks (the client derives the same leg duration the server ticks).
135: * The ship's CURRENT position in the solar system (fleet-anchor coordinates): its launch origin at launch,
136: * then the last leg's endpoint. The client renders the ship here while it HOLDS
137: * ({@link USSShipState#HOVERING}).
141: * Monotonic leg counter (per ship, persisted) — the leg ID. The client resets its leg-progress phase when
142: * this changes, so consecutive legs of the SAME state (MOVE → MOVE) animate from their own start instead of
143: * continuing the previous leg's progress.
147: endregion
152: * The ship's internal cargo hold (the cargo-capacity pass): a bounded hold (capacity in cargo units, where
153: * {@code 1 unit = 1 item = 100 mB}) that mining / starlifting / construction fill. The hold is the ship's
154: * STATE — it is empty at launch, filled by the mission (clamped by capacity), and delivered on return. A full
155: * hold means the ship "cannot mine if it is full" (the yield is 0).
156: *
157: * <p>
158: * The {@link #cargo} NBT (the abstract items + fluids for delivery) is DERIVED from this hold — see
159: * {@link #getCargo()} / {@link #setCargo(NBTTagCompound)}.
164: * The parts loadout a CONSTRUCTOR carries (parts-list key → count on board): filled by the gateway at launch
165: * (from the payload's {@code vc_build_loadout}), consumed PART-BY-PART by the CONSTRUCT legs — a site takes
166: * only what it needs, and whatever is left stays on board (for the next station, or the return to the gateway
167: * when the mission completes).
182: The internal cargo hold (the cargo-capacity pass): empty, capacity from cargoCapacity() (the payload's
183: vc_cargo × CARGO_UNIT_MULTIPLIER — the pass-27 100× hold).
188: The integrity time limit: the ship enters the USS at its MAXIMUM (the blueprint's total) and counts
189: down from there (tickIntegrity()).

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\VoidcraftCargoPool.java ====
8: * The Voidcraft Storage Bay's shared cargo pool: 16 slots (a 4×4 face) that mining ships deliver into and that
9: * hatches move out of.
10: *
11: * <p>
12: * The pool is deliberately <em>not</em> an {@code IInventory}: it is a plain stack array with merge-aware
13: * insert/remove, so the bay MTE drives all bus traffic (input bus → pool, pool → output bus) in
14: * {@code onPostTick} and the pool itself stays pure Java + NBT (unit-testable).
15: *
16: * <p>
17: * Insertion is stack-aware: items merge into an existing compatible slot before taking an empty one (same item id,
18: * same damage, no more than 64 per slot). Overflow that does not fit is simply not inserted — the caller keeps
19: * the remainder (the bay re-pulls it from the input bus next tick).
23: * Slot count (4×4 face).
26: * Max items per slot (vanilla stack size).
42: * Read a pool from NBT (tag written by {@link #writeToNBT()}).
43: *
44: * @param nbt the bay NBT (may be null)
45: * @return the pool (an empty pool for missing/corrupt data — never null)
72: * @param slot slot index 0..15
73: * @return the stack in that slot (null if empty)
83: * @return number of occupied slots
94: * True when every slot is full (no free space and no compatible stack left to merge into).
103: an empty slot always has room
106: a mergeable partial slot has room
108: occupied (full or foreign) slot: no room for this candidate
114: * Insert as much of {@code stack} as fits (merge-aware).
115: *
116: * @param stack the items to insert (mutated: {@code stackSize} reduced by the inserted amount)
117: * @return the number of items actually inserted
124: Pass 1: merge into existing compatible slots.
136: Pass 2: empty slots.
152: * Remove up to {@code amount} items from one slot (merge-agnostic: the caller picks the slot).
153: *
154: * @param slot   slot index 0..15
155: * @param amount items to remove (>= 1)
156: * @return the removed stack (null if the slot was empty), with a stackSize of at most {@code amount}
178: * Remove every item of a given type across the pool (used when the bay is torn down: the stacks are dropped).
179: *
180: * @return a single merged list of everything removed (empty list if the pool was empty)
200: * Serialize the pool (tag for the bay's NBT).
==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\VoidcraftFluidPool.java ====
7: * The Voidcraft Storage Bay's shared FLUID cargo pool (Phase 4 pass 1): 16 slots that Starlifter missions deliver
8: * into and that output hatches (incl. ME output hatches) move out of.
9: *
10: * <p>
11: * Same design contract as {@link VoidcraftCargoPool}, with two deliberate differences:
12: * <ul>
13: * <li>Slots hold <em>abstract</em> entries — {@code {material name, amount (mB)}} — NOT {@code FluidStack}s. The
14: * material name is the stable identity (the Forge fluid registry is a runtime detail; a pool entry must survive a
15: * save/load and stay unit-testable in a bare JVM). The Fluid itself is resolved exactly once at the delivery
16: * boundary (the bay's ejection/pump code, via {@code Materials.get(name).getFluid(mB)}).</li>
17: * <li>The per-slot cap is generous (10_000 mB — one mission carries at most a few tens of thousands of mB), since
18: * fluids are not stack-capped the way items are.</li>
19: * </ul>
20: *
21: * <p>
22: * Insertion is fluid-aware: the same material merges into an existing slot before taking an empty one.
23: * Overflow that does not fit is simply not inserted — the caller keeps the remainder (the bay delivers the
24: * remainder to the hatches on the next tick / at delivery time).
28: * Slot count (mirrors the item pool's 4×4 face).
31: * Max mB per slot (10_000 L — far above a single mission's plasma; keeps 16 slots comfortably bounded).
45: * Read a pool from NBT (tag written by {@link #writeToNBT()}).
46: *
47: * @param nbt the bay NBT (may be null)
48: * @return the pool (an empty pool for missing/corrupt data — never null)
74: * @param slot slot index 0..15
75: * @return the material name in that slot (null if empty)
85: * @param slot slot index 0..15
86: * @return the amount in mB in that slot (0 if empty)
96: * @return number of occupied slots
107: * Insert as much of {@code amount} mB of {@code material} as fits, merging into a compatible slot first.
108: *
109: * @param material GT material name (the fluid's identity in the pool; empty/null rejects the whole amount)
110: * @param amount   mB to insert (&lt;= 0 inserts nothing)
111: * @return the amount accepted (the remainder is up to the caller — the pool never silently voids)
119: 1) merge into an existing slot of the same material
129: 2) take empty slots
145: * Remove up to {@code amount} mB from one slot.
146: *
147: * @param slot   slot index 0..15
148: * @param amount mB to remove
149: * @return the amount actually removed
166: * Serialize the pool (tag for the bay's NBT).
==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\uss\VoidcraftUSS.java ====
13: * The Unstable Solar System model (EoH rework, Phase 2 vertical slice).
14: *
15: * <p>
16: * Holds the star lifecycle: {@link USSState#COLD} until a USS Controller ignites it, then {@link USSState#IGNITED}
17: * with an item-selected star class (Phase 4 pass 1 — the controller item decides the star type) and a ticking-down
18: * lifespan. When the lifespan reaches zero the star burns out, the
19: * controller is consumed (one controller = one star life) and the model returns to {@link USSState#COLD}. The
20: * {@code ships} list is the Phase 3 docking registry — a placeholder for this slice.
21: *
22: * <p>
23: * Pure data + NBT (de)serialization, no Minecraft runtime — so it is unit-testable (see
24: * {@code tectech.voidcraft.uss.VoidcraftUSSTest}). Immutable: state transitions return new instances.
25: *
26: * @see USSConstants
27: * @see docs/Voidcraft_Implementation_Plan.md

42: * Per-planet ore reserves (the mechanics pass — depletion). A list indexed by planet index.
45: * Scanned spacetime-ripple point indices (the Explorer pass — which of the 343 grid points have been revealed).
46: * An int array of point indices (0..342). Empty = nothing scanned yet (all ripples still hidden).
50: * The USS global variable space (programming framework, Phase C) — the "global variable list" ships (and the
51: * future UI / external machines) read and write. A SPARSE list of {@code {i, s}} compounds (only written slots).
54: * Within one planet-reserve compound: the ore list (material + remaining amount).
56: * One ore entry: the GT material name.
58: * One ore entry: the remaining amount (items).
68: * Per-planet ore reserves (the mechanics pass). Index = planet index (stable within a system, since the system
69: * is a pure function of star type + ignition timestamp). A null entry means "not yet mined" (the reserve is
70: * initialized from the planet definition on first mine).
75: * Scanned spacetime-ripple point indices (the Explorer pass). A point is added to this set the moment an
76: * Explorer finishes scanning it (revealing whether it is a ripple). Starts EMPTY — all ripples are hidden at
77: * USS creation (user spec). Immutable: {@link #withRippleScanned} returns a new instance.
82: * The USS global variable space (programming framework, Phase C): 256 string slots shared by the whole fleet —
83: * the ships' in/out channel ("This allows sending commands to Voidcraft externally and communicating data out
84: * of the USS"). Immutable — {@link #withVariables} returns a new instance.
103: * A fresh-space constructor (cold / ignited / toCold — a new system has a fresh variable space).
119: * One planet's remaining ore reserve: a map of GT material → remaining amount (items). Immutable — mining
120: * returns a new instance.

==== E:\Programming\Java\GT5-Unofficial\src\main\java\tectech\voidcraft\VoidcraftTextures.java ====
13: * Resolves each Voidcraft component's block texture exactly once and caches the result forever.
14: *
15: * <p>
16: * <b>Why this class exists:</b> {@code Textures.BlockIcons.custom(name)} deduplicates its
17: * {@code GTCustomBlockIconContainer}s in a name-keyed map — but {@code GTClient.onLoadComplete} calls
18: * {@code Textures.BlockIcons.cleanup()}, which clears that map. Any lookup performed <em>after</em> that
19: * (which is exactly when placed blocks are created, via {@code newMetaEntity}, or when a cover is mounted)
20: * hands back a <em>fresh</em> container whose icon was never registered — the icon load phase has already
21: * run — so {@code getIcon()} returns null and rendering NPEs in {@code GTIconFlipped}.
22: *
23: * <p>
24: * Therefore: the texture must be resolved once, during the load phase (before the icon registration phase),
25: * and that one {@link ITexture} must be reused everywhere forever. This cache is a plain static map that
26: * {@code cleanup()} never touches, so it survives.
37: * PASS 23: the Voidcraft Frame's mostly-transparent framebox texture (a per-face square frame with a
38: * fully-transparent interior — assembled from six faces it reads as a hollow wireframe box).
43: * Cover icon names in {@link VoidcraftCoverComponent#getId()} order — each cover has its OWN 16×16 icon
44: * (pass 24: the old {@code iconsets/EM_DIM_*} names pointed at the IORE dimension-block planet sheets — 16×64
45: * multi-tile textures, which rendered squashed in-world and made covers like the armor plate look
46: * unrendered).
48: * Cover icon basenames in {@link VoidcraftCoverComponent#getId()} order (also exposed for the item icons).
60: * The controller's dedicated block icon (pass 24; was a planet-sheet squashed onto its faces).
63: * Multiblock component block icons — one dedicated 16×16 icon per entry: {@code VC_MBLK_<entry name>}.
67: * The face texture of a cover part — its own dedicated 16×16 icon (also used as the cover ITEM icon, so the
68: * inventory, the mounted face, and the in-flight model all show the same art).
69: *
70: * <p>
71: * The first call must happen during the load phase (see {@link #resolveAll()}); later calls return the cached
72: * instance and must not re-resolve the name.
80: * The registered block-atlas name of a cover's icon — for client code that needs the {@code IIcon} itself
81: * (UVs) rather than the {@link ITexture} (e.g. the in-flight ship model).
88: * The shared block texture of a component, on all faces.
89: *
90: * <p>
91: * Pass 23/24: the two placeable blocks have their own icons (controller panel, framebox), and the seven
92: * cover-only catalog entries map to their cover part's icon (their face is what a mounted cover shows).
93: *
94: * <p>
95: * The first call must happen during the load phase (e.g. when registering the component MTEs); that is when
96: * the icon container gets registered. Later calls simply return the cached instance — they must not
97: * re-resolve the name.
111: Multiblock component block — its own dedicated 16×16 icon (VC_MBLK_<entry name>)
114: cover-only catalog entry — the icon of the cover part that provides this function
124: * Resolves every component and cover texture up front.
125: *
126: * <p>
127: * Must be called during the load phase — before the icon registration phase — so the icon containers are
128: * actually registered. Idempotent; later calls only return cached textures.
